\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{S_{L_{i}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus{E}
which contains
\Gamma_{i}
, see Figure
5
. (From now on, for clarification we again add the index
i
as the definition depends on
u_{i}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U_{i}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\to L_{i}^{\perp}
.) Let us verify (
2.38
)(i). To this end, we observe by (
2.70
)
that

{\partial T_{i}\setminus\overline{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\subset\partial S_{L_{i}}\setminus(-3\eta\rho\nu_{L_{i}}+L_{i})=\partial^{-}S_{L_{i}}\,,}

where the last identity is the definition of
\partial^{-}S_{L_{i}}
. Thus, (
2.74
) implies (
2.38
)(i). We close this step with the observation that for
x\notin E

(2.76)

\displaystyle\dist(x,\Gamma_{i})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\dist\Big(x,\bigcup\nolimits^{M}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=1}{\rm graph}(u_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}j\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\cap\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{Q_{\rho}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Big)<\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta\rho\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\quad\Rightarrow\quad\quad x\in T_{i}\,.

In fact, by using (
2.73
) and by assuming that
\dist(x,\Gamma_{i})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}<\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta\rho\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, we get
x\in S_{L_{i}}
, in particular
x\in{S_{L_{i}}}\setminus E
. If we had
x\in S_{L_{i}}\setminus T_{i}
, then we would necessarily find
\Gamma_{j}
,
j\neq i
, such that
\dist(x,\Gamma_{j})<\dist(x,\Gamma_{i})
, see also Figure
5
. This is a contradiction.

Step 3
.(Bad planes) Now we suppose that
L_{i}
is a
\theta
-bad plane for
Q_{\rho}
. Then, there exists exactly one
k\in\{1,2,3\}
such that
|\nu_{k}|\geq\theta
and
|\nu_{j}|<\theta
for
j\neq k
, and by Lemma
2.21
we find that (
2.67
) holds.
Without restriction we suppose that
k=1
and that
x\in L_{i}\cap Q_{3\rho}
implies that
-\frac{3\rho}{4}\leq x\cdot e_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1}<-\frac{\rho}{4}
. In fact, the other cases can be treated along the very same lines. We let
Q_{\rho}^{\prime}:=Q_{\rho}-\rho e_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\in\mathcal{N}(Q_{\rho})
be the neighboring cube of
Q_{\rho}
to the left of it, recall notation (
2.35
).

Due to (
2.67
), we have that
L_{i}
is a
\theta
-good plane for the shifted cube
\widetilde{Q}_{\rho}:=Q_{\rho}-\frac{\rho}{2}e_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. In fact, Case (2) of Definition
2.19
is satisfied, provided that
\theta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is chosen small enough. Consequently, given (
2.37
) and the fact that
\widetilde{Q}_{8\rho}\subset\Omega
, we can repeat the above reasoning for
\widetilde{Q}_{\rho}
in place of
Q_{\rho}
. Accordingly, we define
\Gamma_{i}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}={\rm graph}(u_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}i\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\cap\widetilde{Q}_{\rho}
and
T_{i}
as the connected component of
\overline{S_{L_{i}}}\setminus{E}
containing
\Gamma_{i}
, where now
S_{L_{i}}=(\widetilde{Q}_{\rho})_{(1+6\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta)\rho}\cap(L_{i})_{3\eta\rho}
. Then (
2.38
)(ii) can be proved along similar lines as (
2.38
)(i) above, by using
\widetilde{Q}_{\rho}\subset Q_{\rho}\cup Q_{\rho}^{\prime}
. In the same way, we obtain (
2.76
) in this case. We now observe that (
2.76
) for good and bad planes yields (
2.39
). In particular, we also note that
(
2.67
) and (
2.74
) imply

(2.77)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\varphi}\big(\mathrm{graph}(u_{i})\cap(Q_{\rho}\cup Q_{\rho}^{\prime})\big)\geq\frac{1}{C}\rho^{2}\,,

provided that
\eta_{0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is chosen sufficiently small.

Next, we confirm (
2.40
). To this end, we exemplarily apply the construction for the neighboring cube
Q_{\rho}^{\prime}=Q_{\rho}-\rho e_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
. By Lemma
2.16
and Remark
2.18
(which are applicable by (
2.37
) and the fact that
Q_{8\rho}^{\prime}\subset\Omega
) we find planes
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}L^{\prime}_{j}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, open sets
U^{\prime}_{j}
in
L^{\prime}_{j}
, and functions
u^{\prime}_{j}
such that (
2.68
)–(
2.70
) hold. Given the
\theta
-bad plane
L_{i}
with corresponding
{\rm graph}(u_{i})
for the original cube
Q_{\rho}
considered above, in view of (
2.68
) applied for both
Q_{\rho}
and
Q_{\rho}^{\prime}
, and by using (
2.77
), we observe that there exists a unique function
u_{j}^{\prime}
such that
{\rm graph}(u_{j}^{\prime})\cap{\rm graph}(u_{i})\cap(Q_{\rho}\cup Q_{\rho}^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
. (In fact, since
\partial E\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is a regular manifold with boundary only in
\partial\Omega
and
\overline{Q_{12\rho}}\subset\Omega
, different graphs cannot intersect and the graphs of the functions in the above representation are unique.) Then we observe that
one could replace
{\rm graph}(u_{i})
and
{\rm graph}(u_{j}^{\prime})
in Lemma
2.16
applied on
Q_{\rho}
and
Q_{\rho}^{\prime}
, respectively, by the union
{\rm graph}(u_{i})\cup{\rm graph}(u_{j}^{\prime})
which can again be understood as the graph of a function defined on the plane
L_{i}
. This shows that the objects
\Gamma_{j}^{\prime}
and
T_{j}^{\prime}
for
L_{j}^{\prime}
can be chosen identical to
\Gamma_{i}
and
T_{i}
, i.e., the sets can indeed be constructed such that (
2.40
) is ensured.

Step 4
.(Presence of pimples)
Now we argue how to reduce the case of existence of pimples to the case of non-existence of pimples. As a preparation, we first show that for every pimple
P_{j}\subset\partial E
such that
P_{j}\cap Q_{3\rho}\neq\emptyset
there exists
i\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
such that
P_{j}\cap\mathrm{graph}(u_{i})\neq\emptyset
. In fact, suppose by contradiction that this was not the case. Due to the fact that Lemma
2.16
guarantees that
P_{k}\cap P_{j}=\emptyset
for all
k\neq j
, we would get that
P_{j}
is a compact manifold without boundary. Thus, applying
[
88
, Lemma 1.1]
we get

\mathcal{H}^{2}(P_{j})\leq({\rm diam}(P_{j}))^{2}\int_{P_{j}}|\bm{H}|^{2}\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\,,

where
\bm{H}
denotes the mean curvature. As the estimate clearly still holds with
\bm{A}
in place of
\bm{H}
up to a factor of
2
, we get along with Hölder’s inequality for
q/2\geq 1
, (
2.1
), (
2.37
), and (
2.69
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(P_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\mathrm{diam}(P_{j}))^{2}\int_{P_{j}}|\bm{A}|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta^{2}\rho^{2}\big(\mathcal{H}^{2}(P_{j})\big)^{1-2/q}\Big(\int_{P_{j}}|\bm{A}|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{2}\Big)^{2/q}

\displaystyle\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta^{2}\rho^{2}\big(\mathcal{H}^{2}(P_{j})\big)^{1-2/q}\big(\Lambda\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{-1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho^{2}\big)^{2/q}\,.

Simplifying the above formula and using the assumption
\rho\leq\eta^{7}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{1/q}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(P_{j})\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2^{q/2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Lambda\gamma^{-1}\rho^{2}\,\rho^{q}\eta^{q}\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2^{q/2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Lambda\eta^{8q}\rho^{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}<\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\rho^{2}\,,

where the last step follows from the fact that
2^{q/2}\Lambda\eta_{0}^{8q}\leq 2^{q/2}\Lambda\eta_{0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}<c_{0}
, see the beginning of the proof for our choice of
\eta_{0}
. By Lemma
2.12
applied to
P_{j}
we would then obtain the estimate
\mathcal{F}_{\rm surf}^{\varphi,\gamma,q}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}E;\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q_{8\rho})\geq\mathcal{F}_{\rm surf}^{\varphi,\gamma,q}(P_{j})>\Lambda\rho^{2}
, where we used that
P_{j}\subset\partial E\cap Q_{8\rho}
, which follows from (
2.69
). This is a contradiction. Therefore, for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
there exists an index
i\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
such that
P_{j}\cap\mathrm{graph}(u_{i})\neq\emptyset
.

Now, omitting again the indices for simplicity, we consider a plane
L
and a function
u\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{U}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\subset L\to L^{\perp}
satisfying (
2.70
), in particular
\|u\|_{\infty}\leq\eta\rho
. Here,
U
is
of the form
U=U^{0}\setminus\bigcup_{k}d_{k}
, where
U^{0}
is a simply connected subdomain of
L
and
(d_{k})_{k}
are pairwise disjoint closed disks in
L
, which do not intersect
\partial U^{0}
. If a pimple
P
touches
{\rm graph}(u)
, it can be covered by a cube that also touches
{\rm graph}(u)
, has normal
\nu_{L}
to one of its faces (the orientation of the others being irrelevant), and sidelength
\mathrm{diam}(P)
. Due to (
2.69
), performing this construction for every pimple, the additional surface introduced by the cubes is bounded by
C\eta^{2}\rho^{2}
for an absolute constant
C>0
. Furthermore, by this procedure we obtain a piecewise smooth function
\widetilde{u}\colon\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\subset L\to L^{\perp}
such that
\|\widetilde{u}\|_{\infty}\leq\|u\|_{\infty}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\max_{j=1}^{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{diam}(P_{j})\leq 2\eta\rho
, i.e., (
2.70
) holds true, where (the classical gradient)
\nabla\widetilde{u}
is well-defined up to a set of
\mathcal{H}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
-measure zero. Additionally, due to the diameter bound on the cubes, see (
2.69
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathrm{graph}(\widetilde{u})\cap Q_{\rho})\leq\mathcal{H}^{2}(\mathrm{graph}(u)\cap Q_{\rho})+C\eta^{2}\rho^{2}\,.

Now Step 2 and Step 3 can be performed for the function
\widetilde{u}
instead of the function
u
in order to conclude the proof.
∎

Remark 2.22

(Obstacles in higher dimensions)
.

We close this section by commenting on the current obstacles to generalize our results to higher dimensions. The two essential ingredients depending crucially on the dimension are Lemma
2.12
and Lemma
2.16
, whereas the rest of our proof strategy can be carried along with very minor modifications. Lemma
2.16
can in some sense be generalized to any dimension
d\geq 2
in the spirit of
\varepsilon
-regularity results
, with respect to the
L^{q}
-norm of the second fundamental form, but for
q>d-1
. The result is due to
Hutchinson
, see
[
5
]
, pages 281-306, in particular Theorem 3.7 on page 295, as well as
[
57
]
, and it is a
graphical representation
rather than an
approximation
result, i.e., the condition
q>d-1
excludes the presence of pimples. As we have seen in Lemma
2.15
, for
d=2
this graphical representation can easily be obtained for every
q\geq 1
, while for
d=3
Simon’s lemma also handles the case
1\leq q\leq 2
, modulo the presence of small pimples. For
d>3
, it would be interesting to investigate to which extent Simon’s lemma can be generalized for
q=d-1
.

The other obstacle to generalize our result to higher dimensions, especially for the critical case
q=d-1
, is Lemma
2.12
. As in the statement of Lemma
2.14
, the main question consists in the validity of the implication that

\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\Sigma\cap B_{R})\geq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c{}_{0}R^{d-1}

for
every connected, regular
(d-1)
-dimensional hypersurface
\Sigma
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\mathcal{H}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d-2\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}(\partial\Sigma\cap\overline{B_{R}})=0
, and

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma\cap B_{R}}|\bm{A}|^{d-2}\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}<\alpha_{0}R,\quad\Sigma\cap\partial B_{R}\neq\emptyset,\quad\text{and}\quad\Sigma\cap\partial B_{\mu R}\neq\emptyset\,,

for suitable
\alpha_{0}=\alpha_{0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu)>0
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c{}_{0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}c{}_{0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu)>0
,
\mu\in(0,1)
. In fact, this would allow us to repeat the proof of Lemma
2.12
for
q\geq d-1
. Whereas the above implication holds true in
d=2
and
d=3
, to the best of our knowledge it is an open question for
d>3
. For related results in higher dimensions, yet not sufficient for our purposes, we refer to
[
85
, Theorem 1.1]
and
[
71
, Theorem A]
.

3.
Applications

This section is devoted to applications of our rigidity result. We identify effective linearized models of nonlinear elastic energies in the small-strain limit in two settings, namely for a model with material voids in elastically stressed solids and for epitaxially strained elastic thin films. In the following, for
d=2,3
we let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain, and
W\colon\mathbb{R}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d\times d\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\to[0,+\infty)
be a frame-indifferent stored elastic energy density with the usual assumptions in nonlinear elasticity. Altogether, we suppose that
W
satisfies the following assumptions

(3.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\rm(i)}&\ \ \text{Frame indifference: $W(RF)=W(F)$ for all $R\in SO(d)$, $F\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}$}\,,\\
{\rm(ii)}&\ \ \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{Single energy-well structure: }\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{W=0\}=SO(d)\,,\\
{\rm(iii)}&\ \ \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{Regularity:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \ \text{$W\in C^{3}$ in a neighborhood of $SO(d)$}\,,\\
{\rm(iv)}&\ \ \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{Coercivity:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \ \text{There exists $c>0$ such that for all $F\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}$ it holds that}\\
&\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\,W(F)\geq c\,\mathrm{dist}^{2}(F,SO(d))\,.\end{split}

Notice that the above assumptions particularly imply that
DW(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\rm Id}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})=0
. The general approach in linearization results in many different settings (see, e.g.,
[
3
,
13
,
32
,
47
,
48
,
75
,
82
,
83
]
) is to consider sequences of deformations
(y_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with small elastic energy, more precisely

\sup\nolimits_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\delta^{-2}\int_{\Omega}W(\nabla y_{\delta})\ \,{\rm d}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}<+\infty\,,

and to pass to the small-strain limit as
\delta\to 0
, in terms of
rescaled displacement fields
, i.e., mappings

(3.2)

\displaystyle u_{\delta}=\frac{1}{\delta}(y_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}-{\rm id}).

These maps measure the distance of the deformations from the identity, rescaled by the typical strain
\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. This yields a linearization of the elastic energy, which can be expressed in terms of the quadratic form
\mathcal{Q}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to[0,+\infty)
defined by

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(F):=D^{2}W({\rm Id})F:F\quad\text{ for all $F\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}$}.

In view of (
3.1
),
\mathcal{Q}
is positive-definite on
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\rm sym}
and vanishes on
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\rm skew}
. We will consider models containing surface energies with an additional curvature regularization as indicated in (
2.1
), where we choose a sequence of scaling parameters
(\gamma_{\delta})_{\delta>0}\subset(0,+\infty)
for which we require

(3.4)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma_{\delta}\to 0\quad\quad\quad\text{and}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\quad\quad\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\left(\delta^{-\frac{q}{3d}}\gamma_{\delta}\right)=+\infty\,.}

In fact, this allows us to define a sequence
(\kappa_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\subset(0,+\infty)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
satisfying

(3.5)

\displaystyle\delta\kappa_{\delta}^{3}\to 0,\quad\quad\quad\gamma_{\delta}^{d/q}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\kappa{}_{\delta}\to\infty\,\quad\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\text{as}\ \ \delta\to 0,

which will play a pivotal role in the linearization procedure.
In the following, we will focus on a curvature regularization in terms of the second fundamental form
\bm{A}
. Under certain assumptions however, in the case
d=3
,
q=2
,
\bm{A}
can be replaced by the mean curvature
\bm{H}
. We refer to Corollaries
3.4
and
3.8
for details in this direction.

In our applications, it will turn out that limiting mappings lie in the space of generalized special functions of bounded deformation
GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
. For basic properties of
GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
, we refer to
[
31
]
and Appendix
A.4
below. In particular, for
u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
, we will denote by
e(u)=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla u+\nabla u^{T})
the approximate symmetric differential and by
J_{u}
the jump set of
u
with measure-theoretical normal
\nu_{u}
. Moreover, by
L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
we denote the space of
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-measurable mappings
v\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, endowed with the topology of the convergence in measure. For any
s\in[0,1]
and any
E\in\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
,
E^{s}
denotes the set of points with
d
-dimensional density
s
with respect to
E
. By
\partial^{*}E
we indicate the essential boundary of
E
, see
[
6
, Definition 3.60]
.

We now present our two applications in Subsections
3.1
–
3.2
. The proofs of the results are deferred to Subsections
3.3
–
3.4
.

3.1.
Material voids in elastically stressed solids

We study boundary value problems for elastically stressed solids with voids. We suppose that the boundary data are imposed on an open subset
\partial_{D}\Omega\subset\partial\Omega
and are close to the identity. To this end, let
u_{0}\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
d=2,3
, and for
\delta>0
define
y_{0}^{\delta}:={\rm id}+\delta u_{0}
. Let further
\varphi
be a norm,
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q\in[d-1,+\infty)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and
(\gamma_{\delta})_{\delta>0}
as in (
3.4
). Then for the density
W\colon\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to[0,\infty)
introduced in (
3.1
), we let
F_{\delta}\colon L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)\to\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[0,+\infty]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
be the functional defined by

(3.6)

F_{\delta}(y,E)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{\Omega\setminus\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{E}}W(\nabla y)\,\,{\rm d}x+\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial E\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega}\varphi(\nu_{E})\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+\gamma_{\delta}\int_{\partial E\cap\Omega}|\bm{A}|^{q}\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\,,

if
E\in\mathcal{A}_{\rm reg}(\Omega)
,
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{E}\cap\partial_{D}\Omega=\emptyset\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,
y|_{\Omega\setminus\overline{E}}\in H^{1}(\Omega\setminus\overline{E};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
y|_{E}={\rm id}
, and
\mathrm{tr}(y)=\mathrm{tr}(y^{\delta}_{0})
on
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{D}\Omega\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and
F_{\delta}(y,E)=+\infty
otherwise. Here,
\nu_{E}
denotes again the outer unit normal to
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial E\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. We emphasize that the energy is determined by
E
and the values of
y
on
\Omega\setminus\overline{E}
. The condition
y|_{E}={\rm id}
is for definiteness only. The relaxation of this model without the curvature regularization term has been studied in
[
12
,
81
]
. Here, instead, we are interested in an effective description in the small-strain limit
\delta\to 0
, in terms of displacement fields defined in (
3.2
). From now on, we write

\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(u,E):=F_{\delta}({\rm id}+\delta u,E)

for notational convenience. We start with a compactness result which fundamentally relies on Theorem
2.1
. Note that in what follows, the sets
\omega_{u}^{\delta},\omega_{u}
serve a totally different purpose, and should not be confused with the set
\omega_{u}^{\rho}
in Section
2
, see for instance (
2.66
).

Proposition 3.1

(Compactness, void case)
.

For every sequence of pairs
(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})_{\delta>0}
with

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M:=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sup\nolimits_{\delta>0}\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})<+\infty,

there exist a subsequence (not relabeled),
u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
, sets of finite perimeter
E\in\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
,
(E_{\delta}^{*})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0}\subset\mathfrak{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
E_{\delta}\subset E_{\delta}^{*}
, as well as sets
\omega_{u},(\omega_{u}^{\delta})_{\delta>0}\subset\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
such that
u\equiv 0
on
E\cup\omega_{u}
,

\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}\omega_{u})+\sup_{\delta>0}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}\omega_{u}^{\delta})\leq C_{M}

for a constant
C_{M}>0
depending only on
M
, and as
\delta\to 0
,

(3.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\rm(i)}&\ \ u_{\delta}\to u\text{ in measure on $\Omega\setminus\omega_{u}$}\,,\\
{\rm(ii)}&\ \ \chi_{\Omega\setminus(E^{*}_{\delta}\cup\omega^{\delta}_{u})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e(u_{\delta})\rightharpoonup\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega\setminus(E\cup\omega_{u})}e(u)\ \ \text{weakly in $L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\rm sym})$}\,,\\
{\rm(iii)}&\ \ \mathcal{L}^{d}(\{|\nabla u_{\delta}|>\kappa_{\delta}\}\setminus\omega_{u})\to 0\,,\\
{\rm(iv)}&\ \ \liminf_{\delta\to 0}\int_{\partial{E_{\delta}^{*}}\cap\Omega}\varphi(\nu_{E^{*}_{\delta}})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\leq\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{F}_{\rm surf}^{\varphi,\gamma_{\delta},q}(E_{\delta})\,,\\
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\rm(v)}&\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \ \lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{L}^{d}(\omega_{u}^{\delta}\triangle\omega_{u})=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{L}^{d}(E_{\delta}^{*}\setminus E_{\delta})=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{L}^{d}(E_{\delta}\triangle E)=0\,,\end{split}

where
\kappa_{\delta}
is defined in (
3.5
) and
\mathcal{F}_{\rm surf}^{\varphi,\gamma_{\delta},q}
in (
2.1
).

In the following, we say that a sequence
(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\subset L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\times\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
converges to a pair
(u,E)\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}d\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\times\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
in the
\tau
-sense and write
(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})\overset{\tau}{\to}(u,E)
iff there exists a set
\omega_{u}\in\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
such that
\chi_{E_{\delta}}\to\chi_{E}\text{ in }L^{1}(\Omega)
,
u_{\delta}\to u
in measure on
\Omega\setminus\omega_{u}
, and
u\equiv 0
on
E\cup\omega_{u}
.

The compactness result is non-standard in the sense that the behavior of the sequence
(u_{\delta})_{\delta>0}
on
\omega_{u}
cannot be controlled. This set is related to the fact that
\Omega\setminus\overline{E_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta}}
might be disconnected into various connected components
(P^{\delta}_{j})_{j}
by
E_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, and on the sets not intersecting
\partial_{D}\Omega
the corresponding rotations
R^{\delta}_{j}
, obtained from (
2.3
), cannot be controlled. It is however essential that
|R^{\delta}_{j}-{\rm Id}|
is at most of order
\delta
, as otherwise
u_{\delta}
defined in (
3.2
) blows up on
P^{\delta}_{j}
. In this sense, roughly speaking,
\omega^{\delta}_{u}
consists of the components
(P^{\delta}_{j})_{j}
not intersecting
\partial_{D}\Omega
. Moreover, the sets
E_{\delta}
need to be replaced by the slightly larger sets
E_{\delta}^{*}
corresponding to the sets in (
2.2
).

We now introduce the linearized model studied in
[
29
]
.
Given
u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
and
E\in\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
with
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}E)<+\infty
, we first define the
boundary energy term
by

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\rm bd\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}(u,E):=\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial^{*}E\cap\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{D}\Omega}\varphi(\nu_{E})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+\int\limits_{\{\mathrm{tr}(u)\neq\mathrm{tr}(u_{0})\}\cap(\partial_{D}\Omega\setminus\partial^{*}E)}\hskip-14.22636pt2\,\varphi(\nu_{\Omega})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1},

which is nontrivial if the void goes up to the Dirichlet part of the boundary or the mapping
u
does not satisfy the imposed boundary conditions. Here,
\nu_{\Omega}
denotes the outer unit normal to
\partial\Omega
, and
{\rm tr}(u)
indicates the trace of
u
at
\partial\Omega
, which is well defined for functions in
GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
, see Appendix
A.4
. Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{Q}
in (
3.3
), we introduce the
effective limiting energy

\mathcal{F}_{0}\colon L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})\times\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)\to[0,+\infty]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
by

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{0}(u,E)

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega\setminus E}\mathcal{Q}(e(u))\,\mathrm{d}x+\int\limits_{\partial^{*}E\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\hskip-5.69046pt\varphi(\nu_{E})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+\int\limits_{J_{u}\setminus\partial^{*}E}\hskip-5.69046pt2\,\varphi(\nu_{u})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+\mathcal{F}^{\rm bd\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}(u,E)

if
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}E)<+\infty
and
u=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega\setminus E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
, and
\mathcal{F}_{0}(u,E)=+\infty
otherwise.

We now address that (
3.9
) can be identified as the
\Gamma
-limit of (
3.6
) for
\delta\to 0
. In fact, the functional (
3.9
) is effective in two respects: first, in the small-strain limit the density of nonlinear elasticity is replaced by its linearized version
\mathcal{Q}
. Secondly, the fact that
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}
is not lower semicontinuous in the variable
E
with respect to
L^{1}
-convergence of sets is remedied by a suitable relaxation. Indeed, in the limiting process, the voids
E
may collapse into a discontinuity of the displacement
u
. In particular, this phenomenon is taken into account in the relaxed functional since collapsed surfaces are counted twice in the surface energy. Eventually, we point out that, due to the fact that
\gamma_{\delta}\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
, the curvature regularization of the nonlinear energy
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}
does not affect the linearized limit.

For the
\Gamma
-limsup inequality, more precisely for the application of a density result in
GSBD^{2}
, see
[
29
, Lemma 5.7]
, we make the following geometrical assumption on the Dirichlet boundary
\partial_{D}\Omega
: there exists a decomposition
\partial\Omega=\partial_{D}\Omega\cup\partial_{N}\Omega\cup N
with

\displaystyle\partial_{D}\Omega,\partial_{N}\Omega\text{ relatively open},\ \ \ \mathcal{H}^{d-1}(N)=0,\ \ \ \partial_{D}\Omega\cap\partial_{N}\Omega=\emptyset,\ \ \ \partial(\partial_{D}\Omega)=\partial(\partial_{N}\Omega),

where the outermost boundary has to be understood in the relative sense, and there exist
\bar{\sigma}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
small enough and
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for all
\sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in(0,\bar{\sigma}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
it holds that

\displaystyle O_{\sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0},x_{0}}(\partial_{D}\Omega)\subset\Omega,

where
O_{\sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0},x_{0}}(x):=x_{0}+(1-\sigma\color[rgb]{0,0,0})(x-x_{0})
. Recall the convergence
\tau
introduced below Proposition
3.1
.

Theorem 3.2

(
\Gamma
-convergence, void case)
.

Under the above assumptions, as
\delta\to 0
, we have that the sequence of functionals
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{F}_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}){}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\Gamma
-converges to
\mathcal{F}_{0}
with respect to the convergence
\tau
.

Remark 3.3

(Volume of voids)
.

(i) In the previous result, if
\mathcal{L}^{d}(E)>0
, then for any
(u,E)\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}){\times}\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
there exists a recovery sequence
(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\subset L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}){\times}\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
such that
\mathcal{L}^{d}(E_{\delta})=\mathcal{L}^{d}(E)
for all
\delta>0
. This shows that it is possible to incorporate a volume constraint on
E
in the
\Gamma
-convergence result.

(ii) If we impose the condition
\mathcal{L}^{d}(E_{\delta})\to 0
along the sequence, we obtain
E=\emptyset
, and the limiting model corresponds to an (anisotropic)
Griffith energy of brittle fracture
.

We address an alternative formulation with the mean curvature in place of the second fundamental form, in the case
d=3
,
q=2
.

Corollary 3.4

(Mean curvature regularization)
.

We consider (
3.6
) with
|\bm{H}|^{2}
in place of
|\bm{A}|^{2}
when
d=3
,
q=2
. We suppose that for
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}
, only sets
E
satisfying
E\subset\subset\Omega
and
-4\pi\chi(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial\color[rgb]{0,0,0}E)\leq\lambda_{\delta}\gamma_{\delta}^{-1}

for some
\lambda_{\delta}\to 0
are admissible, where
\chi(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial\color[rgb]{0,0,0}E)
indicates the Euler characteristic of
\partial E
. (For instance, this holds if
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial E
consists of connected components topologically equivalent to the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.) Then, the statements of Proposition
3.1
and Theorem
3.2
hold.

3.2.
Energies on domains with a subgraph constraint: epitaxially strained films

We now address a second application, namely deformations of an elastic material in a domain which is the subgraph of an unknown nonnegative function
h
. Assuming that
h
is defined on a smooth bounded domain
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
,
d=2,3
, deformations
y
will be defined on the
subgraph

\Omega_{h}^{+}:=\{x\in\omega\times\mathbb{R}\colon 0<x_{d}<h(x^{\prime})\},

where here and in the following we use the notation
x=(x^{\prime},x_{d})
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. To model Dirichlet boundary data on the flat surface
\omega\times\{0\}
, we will suppose that mappings are extended to the set
\Omega_{h}:=\{x\in\omega\times\mathbb{R}\colon-1<x_{d}<h(x^{\prime})\}
and satisfy
y=y^{\delta}_{0}:={\rm id}+\delta u_{0}
on
\omega{\times}(-1,0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
for a given function
u_{0}\in W^{1,\infty}(\omega{\times}(-1,0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In the application to epitaxially strained films,
y^{\delta}_{0}
represents the interaction with the substrate and
h
indicates the profile of the free surface of the film. We refer to
[
9
,
21
,
29
]
for a thorough description of the model and a detailed account of the available literature.

For convenience, we introduce the reference domain
\Omega:=\omega{\times}(-1,M+1)
for some
M>0
. For
q\in[d-1,+\infty)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,
\gamma_{\delta}
as in (
3.4
), and the density
W\colon\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to[0,\infty)
introduced in (
3.1
), we define the energy
G_{\delta}\colon L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})\times L^{1}(\omega;[0,M])\to\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[0,+\infty]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
by

(3.10)

G_{\delta}(y,h)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\int_{\Omega_{h}^{+}}W(\nabla y(x))\,\,{\rm d}x+\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\big(\partial\Omega_{h}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega\big)+\gamma_{\delta}\int_{\partial\Omega_{h}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega}|\bm{A}|^{q}\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\,,

if
h\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C^{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\omega;[0,M])
,
y|_{\Omega_{h}}\in H^{1}(\Omega_{h};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
y={\rm id}
in
\Omega\setminus\overline{\Omega_{h}}
,
y=y^{\delta}_{0}
in
\omega{\times}(-1,0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and
G_{\delta}(y,h):=+\infty
otherwise. We emphasize that the two surface terms only contribute in terms of the upper surface
\partial\Omega_{h}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega
of the film, which exactly corresponds to the graph of
h
. In other words, the first surface term is exactly
\int_{\omega}\sqrt{1+|\nabla h(x^{\prime})|^{2}}\,\,{\rm d}x^{\prime}
. On the other hand, the curvature term can be written as
\int_{\omega}|\nabla^{2}h(x^{\prime})|^{q}(1+|\nabla h(x^{\prime})|^{2})^{\frac{1-q}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}
. Note that this model can be seen as a special case of (
3.6
) when we choose
E=\Omega\setminus\overline{\Omega_{h}}
. As in Subsection
3.1
, the assumption
y={\rm id}
in
\Omega\setminus\overline{\Omega_{h}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is for definiteness only.

The relaxation of this model has been studied in
[
21
]
. Notice that, in contrast to
[
9
,
21
]
, here we assume that the functions
h
are equibounded by a value
M
: this is for technical reasons only and is justified from a mechanical point of view, as indeed other physical effects come into play for very high crystal profiles. In the present work, we address the effective behavior of the model in the small-strain limit
\delta\to 0
, again in terms of displacement fields as defined in (
3.2
). From now on, we write

\mathcal{G}_{\delta}(u,h)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=G_{\delta}({\rm id}+\delta u,h)

for notational convenience. Based on Theorem
2.1
, we obtain the following compactness result.

Proposition 3.5

(Compactness, graph case)
.

For any sequence of pairs
(u_{\delta},h_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K:=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\sup\nolimits_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}(u_{\delta},h_{\delta})<+\infty,}

there exist a subsequence (not relabeled), sets of finite perimeter
(E_{\delta}^{*})_{\delta>0}\subset\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
with
\Omega\setminus\overline{\Omega_{h_{\delta}}}\subset E_{\delta}^{*}
, as well as
(\omega_{u}^{\delta})_{\delta>0}\subset\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
, and functions
u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
,
h\in BV(\omega;[0,M])
with
u=\chi_{\Omega_{h}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u
and
u=u_{0}
on
\omega\times(-1,0]
such that

\sup_{\delta>0}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}\omega_{u}^{\delta})\leq C_{K}

for a constant
C_{K}>0
depending only on
K
, and as
\delta\to 0
,

(3.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\rm(i)}&\ \ u_{\delta}\to u\text{ in measure on $\Omega$}\,,\\
{\rm(ii)}&\ \ \chi_{\Omega\setminus(E^{*}_{\delta}\cup\omega_{u}^{\delta})}e(u_{\delta})\rightharpoonup e(u)=\chi_{\Omega_{h}}e(u)\ \ \text{weakly in $L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\rm sym})$}\,,\\
{\rm(iii)}&\ \ \mathcal{L}^{d}(\{|\nabla u_{\delta}|>\kappa_{\delta}\})\to 0\,,\\
{\rm(iv)}&\ \ \liminf\nolimits_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial E^{*}_{\delta}\cap\Omega)\leq\liminf\nolimits_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{F}_{\rm surf}^{q,\delta}(E_{\delta})\,,\\
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\rm(v)}&\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\ \ \lim_{\delta\to 0}\|h_{\delta}-h\|_{L^{1}(\omega)}=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{L}^{d}(\omega_{u}^{\delta})=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{L}^{d}(E_{\delta}^{*}\cap\Omega_{h_{\delta}})=0\,,\end{split}

where
\kappa_{\delta}
is defined in (
3.5
), and
\mathcal{F}_{\rm surf}^{q\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
in (
2.1
) for
\varphi\equiv 1
and
\gamma=\gamma_{\delta}
.

We note that in contrast to Proposition
3.1
no exceptional set
\omega_{u}
is needed here. Indeed, in this setting we obtain a stronger compactness result due to the graph constraint on
\partial\Omega^{+}_{h_{\delta}}\cap\Omega
.

We now introduce the effective model studied in
[
29
]
. Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{Q}
in (
3.3
), we introduce
\mathcal{G}_{0}\colon L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})\times L^{1}(\omega;[0,M])\to\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[0,+\infty]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
by

(3.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{0}(u,h)

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}:\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega_{h}^{+}}\mathcal{Q}(e(u))\,\mathrm{d}x+\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}\Omega_{h}\cap\Omega)+2\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(J_{u}^{\prime}\cap\Omega_{h}^{1})

if
u=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega_{h}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
,
u=u_{0}\text{ in }\omega{\times}(-1,0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,
h\in BV(\omega;[0,M])
, and
\mathcal{G}_{0}(u,h)=+\infty
otherwise. Here,
e(u)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla u+\nabla u^{T}\right)
again denotes the symmetric part of the (approximate) gradient of
u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
,
\Omega^{1}_{h}
denotes the set of points with density
1
, and

(3.13)

\displaystyle J_{u}^{\prime}:=\{(x^{\prime},x_{d}+t)\colon\,x\in J_{u},\,t\geq 0\}\,.

As for the functional (
3.9
), the energy (
3.12
) is effective in the sense that the elastic energy density
W
is replaced by the linearized density
\mathcal{Q}
and the model accounts for “vertical cuts”
J_{u}^{\prime}\cap\Omega^{1}_{h}
(see
[
40
]
) which may appear along the relaxation process. Similarly to the corresponding term in (
3.9
), this part is counted twice in the energy. The set
(\partial^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}*}\Omega_{h}\cap\Omega)\cup\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(J_{u}^{\prime}\cap\Omega^{1}_{h})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
can be interpreted as a “generalized interface”, cf. Figure
6
for a two dimensional section of a possible limiting
\Omega_{h}
. As before, due to the fact that
\gamma_{\delta}\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
, the curvature regularization of the nonlinear energy
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
does not affect the linearized limit.

We work under the additional assumption that
\omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
is uniformly star-shaped with respect to the origin, i.e.,

\displaystyle tx\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\omega\ \ \ \text{for all}\ \ t\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0,1),\,x\in\partial\omega.

This condition, however, is only of technical nature and could be dropped at the expense of more elaborated estimates, see also
[
21
,
29
]
. We obtain the following result.

Theorem 3.6

(
\Gamma
-convergence, graph case)
.

Under the above assumptions, as
\delta\to 0
, we have that the sequence of functionals
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{G_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}){}_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\Gamma
-converges to the functional
\mathcal{G}_{0}
with respect to the topology of
L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d}){\times}L^{1}(\omega;[0,M])
.

Remark 3.7

(Volume constraint)
.

We note that along the linearization process one could consider an additional volume constraint on the film, i.e.,
\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega_{h}^{+})=\int_{\omega}h(x^{\prime})\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}
is fixed.

\omega\times(-1,0)
\partial^{*}\Omega_{h}\cap\Omega
\omega
h
J_{u}^{\prime}\cap\Omega_{h}^{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

Figure 6.
Possible limiting set
\Omega_{h}
.

We close this section with a result for an alternative setting where in (
3.10
) the second fundamental form is replaced by the mean curvature, again in the case
d=3
,
q=2
.

Corollary 3.8

(Mean curvature regularization)
.

We consider (
3.10
) with
|\bm{H}|^{2}
in place of
|\bm{A}|^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
when
d=3
,
q=2
. We suppose that for
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}
only functions
h
are admissible such that
\Gamma_{h}:=\partial\Omega_{h}\cap\Omega
satisfies that
\partial\Gamma_{h}
is
C^{2}
and that

\int_{\partial\Gamma_{h}}\kappa_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h,g\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}1\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\leq\lambda_{\delta}\gamma_{\delta}^{-1}

for some
\lambda_{\delta}\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
, where
\kappa_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h,g\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
denotes the geodesic curvature of
\partial\Gamma_{h}
. Then, the statements of Proposition
3.5
and Theorem
3.6
hold.

The next subsections are devoted to the proofs announced in this section. As the proofs for both applications are similar, we proceed simultaneously. We first address the compactness statements in Subsection
3.3
, and afterwards the
\Gamma
-convergence results in Subsection
3.4
.

3.3.
Compactness results

We start with the proof of Proposition
3.1
. Afterwards, we present the small adaptions necessary for the proof of Proposition
3.5
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Consider a sequence
(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})_{\delta>0}
with
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M<+\infty\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
for all
\delta>0
, where
M>0
is defined in the statement of the proposition. Hence,
\partial E_{\delta}\cap\partial_{D}\Omega=\emptyset
and, as
\min_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\varphi>0
, it holds that
\sup_{\delta>0}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial E_{\delta})<+\infty
. Thus, a compactness result for sets of finite perimeter (see
[
6
, Theorem 3.39]
) implies that there exists a set of finite perimeter
E\subset\Omega
with
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}E)<+\infty
such that
\chi_{E_{\delta}}\to\chi_{E}
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
, up to a subsequence (not relabeled).This shows the last part of (
3.7
)(v).

We now proceed with the compactness for the deformations. We start by introducing sets for a suitable formulation of the Dirichlet boundary conditions: choose an open set
V\supset\Omega
such that
V
and
V\setminus\overline{\Omega}
are Lipschitz sets and
V\cap\partial\Omega=\partial_{D}\Omega
. Our goal is to apply Theorem
2.1
in the version of Corollary
2.2
for
U:=\Omega
and
U_{D}:=V\setminus{\Omega}
. To this end, we introduce the functions
\hat{y}_{\delta}
by

(3.14)

\displaystyle\hat{y}_{\delta}=\begin{cases}{\rm id}+\delta(u_{\delta}-u_{0})&\text{ on $U=\Omega$},\\
{\rm id}&\text{ on $U_{D}=V\setminus\Omega$}\,.\end{cases}

Note that
\hat{y}_{\delta}
are Sobolev functions when restricted to
V\setminus\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{E_{\delta}}
since
V\cap\partial\Omega=\partial_{D}\Omega
and
\mathrm{tr}(\hat{y}_{\delta})=\mathrm{tr}(y^{\delta}_{0}-\delta u_{0})={\rm id}
on
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{D}\Omega\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, by the fact that
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})<+\infty
. Then, by the triangle inequality, (
3.1
), and the fact that
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, we get that

(3.15)

\displaystyle\int_{V\setminus\overline{E}}\dist^{2}\big(\nabla\hat{y}_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}(x),SO(d)\big)\,{\rm d}x\leq C^{\prime}\delta^{2}

for a constant
C^{\prime}>0
depending on
M
and also on
u_{0}
. We want to apply Theorem
2.1
on
(\hat{y}_{\delta},E_{\delta})
. To this end, in view of the fact that
\gamma_{\delta}\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
, see (
3.4
), and the definition of
\kappa_{\delta}
in (
3.5
), by a suitable diagonal argument we can find a sequence
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\eta_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}){}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with
\eta_{\delta}\to 0
and smooth sets
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\subset\subset V
such that, as
\delta\to 0
,

(3.16)

\displaystyle{\rm(i)}

\displaystyle\ \ C_{\eta_{\delta}}\kappa_{\delta}^{-2}\gamma_{\delta}^{-2d/q}\to 0,\quad\quad{\rm(ii)}\ \ \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sup\nolimits_{\delta>0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\eta_{\delta}}\delta^{1/3}<+\infty\,,

(3.17)

\displaystyle{\rm(i)}

\displaystyle\ \ \mathcal{L}^{d}(V\setminus\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta})\to 0,\quad\quad{\rm(ii)}\ \ \sup\nolimits_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta})<+\infty\,,

where
C_{\eta_{\delta}}
is the constant in (
2.3
). We then apply Theorem
2.1
for
\eta_{\delta}
and
\gamma_{\delta}
, for
V
in place of
\Omega
, and for
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta}
in place of
\widetilde{\Omega}
. We use the notation
\mathcal{F}_{\rm surf}^{\varphi,\gamma_{\delta},q}
introduced in (
2.1
). Since
E_{\delta}\subset\Omega
,
V\cap\partial\Omega=\partial_{D}\Omega
and
\overline{E_{\delta}}\cap\partial_{D}\Omega=\emptyset
, we have
E_{\delta}\in\mathcal{A}_{\rm{reg}}(V)
and
\mathcal{F}_{\rm surf}^{\varphi,\gamma_{\delta},q}(E_{\delta},V)=\mathcal{F}_{\rm surf}^{\varphi,\gamma_{\delta},q}(E_{\delta})\leq C^{\prime}
for every
\delta>0
. Now, by applying (
2.2
)–(
2.3
) and using that
\gamma_{\delta}\to 0
,
\eta_{\delta}\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
, we get that there exist sets
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}E^{*}_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}){}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with
E_{\delta}\subset E^{*}_{\delta}\subset\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,
\partial E^{*}_{\delta}\cap\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is a union of finitely many regular submanifolds for every
\delta>0
, and

(3.18)

\displaystyle{\rm(i)}\ \ \lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{L}^{d}(E^{*}_{\delta}\setminus E_{\delta})=0\,,\quad\quad\quad{\rm(ii)}\ \liminf_{\delta\to 0}\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial E^{*}_{\delta}\cap V}\varphi(\nu_{E^{*}_{\delta}})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\leq\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{F}_{\rm surf}^{\varphi,\gamma_{\delta},q}(E_{\delta})\,,

such that for the finitely many connected components of
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta}\setminus E^{*}_{\delta}
, denoted by
(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\Omega}{}^{\eta_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\gamma_{\delta}}_{j})_{j}
, there exist corresponding rotations
(R^{\eta_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\gamma_{\delta}}_{j})_{j}\subset SO(d)
such that by (
3.15
)

(3.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\rm(i)}&\ \ \sum\nolimits_{j}\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\Omega}{}^{\eta_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\gamma_{\delta}}_{j}}\big|{\rm sym}\big((R^{\eta_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\gamma_{\delta}}_{j})^{T}\nabla\hat{y}_{\delta}-\mathrm{Id}\big)\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C_{0}C^{\prime}\delta^{2},\\
{\rm(ii)}&\ \ \sum\nolimits_{j}\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\Omega}{}^{\eta_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\gamma_{\delta}}_{j}}\big|(R^{\eta_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\gamma_{\delta}}_{j})^{T}\nabla\hat{y}_{\delta}-\mathrm{Id}\big|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\eta_{\delta}}\gamma_{\delta}^{-2d/q}\delta^{2}\,.\end{split}

In fact, for (
3.19
)(i) we used that
C_{\eta_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma_{\delta}^{-5d/q}\delta^{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=(\delta^{-q/3d}\gamma_{\delta})^{-5d/q}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\eta_{\delta}}\delta^{1/3}\to 0
by (
3.4
) and (
3.16
)(ii). In view of Corollary
2.2
and (
3.14
), we can choose
R^{\eta_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\gamma_{\delta}}_{j}={\rm Id}
whenever we have
\mathcal{L}^{d}(U_{D}\cap\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\Omega}{}^{\eta_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\gamma_{\delta}}_{j})>0
. We denote the union of the components with this property by
\Omega_{\delta}^{\rm good}
. Note that

(3.20)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\delta}^{\rm good}\supset(U_{D}\cap\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta})\setminus E^{*}_{\delta}\,.

We introduce the mappings
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}){}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\in GSBD^{2}(V)
by

(3.21)

\displaystyle v_{\delta}=\begin{cases}u_{\delta}&\text{ on $\Omega_{\delta}^{\rm good}\cap\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Omega\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$},\\
u_{0}&\text{ on $\Omega_{\delta}^{\rm good}\cap\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(V\setminus{\Omega})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$},\\
0&\text{ on $E^{*}_{\delta}\cup(V\setminus\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta})$},\\
\frac{1}{\delta}e_{1}&\text{ on $\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta}\setminus(\Omega_{\delta}^{\rm good}\cup E^{*}_{\delta})$}\,,\end{cases}

where
e_{1}
denotes the first coordinate vector, see Figure
7
for the different regions in the definition of
v_{\delta}
. By (
3.14
), (
3.19
), (
3.21
), the definition of
\Omega_{\delta}^{\rm good}
and the triangle inequality, we find for all
\delta>0
that

(3.22)

\displaystyle{\rm(i)}\ \ \|e(v_{\delta})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(V)}\leq C^{\prime},\quad\quad\quad{\rm(ii)}\ \ \|\nabla v_{\delta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(V)}\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\eta_{\delta}}\gamma_{\delta}^{-2d/q}\,,

where
C^{\prime}
depends additionally on
u_{0}
.

V\setminus\Omega
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta}^{\mathrm{good}}
\Omega
\partial_{D}\Omega
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta}
E^{*}_{\delta}

Figure 7.
The sets relevant for the definition of
v_{\delta}
: the thick curve indicates the set
\partial_{D}\Omega
and
E_{\delta}^{*}
is depicted in gray. The region delimited by the dashed curve is
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta}
. The region enclosed by the dotted curve is
V\setminus\Omega
. The set
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta}^{\mathrm{good}}
is depicted in light gray.

As
J_{v_{\delta}}\subset\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\partial E^{*}_{\delta}\cap V)\cup\partial\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta}
, (
3.17
)(ii), (
3.18
), (
3.22
), and the fact that
\min_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\varphi>0
imply that

\sup\nolimits_{\delta>0}\big(\|e(v_{\delta})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(V)}+\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(J_{v_{\delta}})\big)<+\infty\,.

By a compactness result in
GSBD^{2}
, see Theorem
A.3
, letting

(3.23)

\displaystyle\omega_{u}:=\{x\in V\colon|v_{\delta}(x)|\to\infty\text{ as }\delta\to 0\}\,,

we get that
\omega_{u}
is a set of finite perimeter, and we find
v\in GSBD^{2}(V)
with
v=0
on
\omega_{u}
such that (again up to a subsequence, not relabeled)
v_{\delta}
converges in measure to
v
on
V\setminus\omega_{u}
. (In the language of
[
29
, Subsection 3.4]
we say that
v_{\delta}\to v
weakly in
GSBD^{2}_{\infty}(V)
.) Moreover, we note that
v=0
a.e. on
E
which follows from the convergence in measure, the fact that
\chi_{E_{\delta}}\to\chi_{E}
, (
3.18
)(i), and (
3.21
). Thus,
v=0
a.e. on
E\cup\omega_{u}
. We also find that

(3.24)

\displaystyle v=u_{0}\quad\text{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}a.e.\ \color[rgb]{0,0,0}on $U_{D}=V\setminus\Omega$}

by (
3.17
)(i), (
3.18
)(i), (
3.20
), (
3.21
), and the fact that
E\subset\Omega
. (Here and in the following, set inclusions will be intended in the measure-theoretical sense, i.e., up to sets of
\mathcal{L}^{d}
-measure zero.) Therefore, we get
\omega_{u}\subset U=\Omega
. We denote the restriction of
v
to
\Omega
by
u
, and note that then
u=0
on
E\cup\omega_{u}
. We also observe that

(3.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{d}\big(\Omega\setminus(\omega_{u}\cup\Omega_{\delta}^{\rm good}\cup E_{\delta})\big)\to 0\quad\text{ as $\delta\to 0$}\,.

In fact,
\mathcal{L}^{d}(\Omega\setminus\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta})\to 0
by (
3.17
)(i),
\mathcal{L}^{d}(E^{*}_{\delta}\setminus E_{\delta})\to 0
by (
3.18
)(i), and
\mathcal{L}^{d}((\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta}\setminus(\Omega_{\delta}^{\rm good}\cup E^{*}_{\delta}))\setminus\omega_{u})\to 0
by (
3.21
) and (
3.23
).

We now show properties (
3.7
). First of all, (
3.7
)(iv) follows directly from (
3.18
)(ii). Since
\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})<+\infty
for all
\delta>0
, we have
u_{\delta}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega\setminus E_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{\delta}
. Then, using (
3.21
) as well as (
3.25
), we get that
\mathcal{L}^{d}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Omega\setminus\omega_{u})\cap\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{v_{\delta}\neq u_{\delta}\})\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
and thus
u_{\delta}\to v=u
in measure on
\Omega\setminus\omega_{u}
. This shows (
3.7
)(i). To see (
3.7
)(iii), we again use that
\mathcal{L}^{d}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Omega\setminus\omega_{u})\cap\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{v_{\delta}\neq u_{\delta}\})\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
, as well as (
3.16
)(i) and (
3.22
)(ii) to calculate

\displaystyle\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{L}^{d}\big(\{|\nabla u_{\delta}|>\kappa_{\delta}\}\setminus\omega_{u}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{L}^{d}\big(\{|\nabla v_{\delta}|>\kappa_{\delta}\}\setminus\omega_{u}\big)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\kappa_{\delta}^{-2}\int_{V}|\nabla v_{\delta}|^{2}\,dx\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

\displaystyle\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C^{\prime}\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{\eta_{\delta}}\kappa_{\delta}^{-2}\gamma_{\delta}^{-2d/q}=0\,.

It therefore remains to define the sets
(\omega_{u}^{\delta})_{\delta>0}\subset\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
and to prove (
3.7
)(ii),(v). Let
V^{\prime}\subset\subset V
. Since
\chi_{E_{\delta}}\to\chi_{E}
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
, (
3.18
)(i) also implies that
\chi_{E^{*}_{\delta}}\to\chi_{E}
in
L^{1}(V)
. Moreover, for
\delta>0
small depending also on
V^{\prime}
, we have
v_{\delta}=0
on
E^{*}_{\delta}
and
v_{\delta}|_{V^{\prime}\setminus\overline{E^{*}_{\delta}}}\in H^{1}(V^{\prime}\setminus\overline{E^{*}_{\delta}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, see (
3.17
)(i) and (
3.21
). This along with the fact that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})_{\delta>0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
converges weakly to
v
in
GSBD^{2}_{\infty}(V)
, means that we can apply
[
29
, Theorem 5.1]
on the set
V^{\prime}
for
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})_{\delta>0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}E^{*}_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})_{\delta>0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
to find

(3.26)

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{V^{\prime}\setminus(E^{*}_{\delta}\cup\omega_{u})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e(v_{\delta})\rightharpoonup\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{V^{\prime}\setminus(E\cup\omega_{u})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e(v)\quad\text{ weakly in }L^{2}(V^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\rm sym})\,,

as well as

(3.27)

\int_{J_{v}\cap E^{0}\cap V^{\prime}}2\varphi(\nu_{v})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial^{*}E\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap V^{\prime}}\varphi(\nu_{E})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\leq\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\int_{\partial E^{*}_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap V^{\prime}}\varphi(\nu_{E^{*}_{\delta}})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\,,

where
E^{0}
denotes the set of points with density zero for
E
and
\nu_{v}
is a measure-theoretical unit normal to
J_{v}
. Define
\omega_{u}^{\delta}:=\omega_{u}\cup(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Omega\cap\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\delta})\setminus(\Omega_{\delta}^{\rm good}\cup E_{\delta}^{*}))
. Note that
\mathcal{L}^{d}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\omega^{\delta}_{u}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\triangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\omega_{u}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\to 0
by (
3.18
)(i) and (
3.25
), which finishes the verification of (
3.7
)(v), and that

\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}\omega_{u})+\sup_{\delta>0}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}\omega_{u}^{\delta})\leq C_{M}

by (
3.17
)(ii), (
3.18
)(ii), and Theorem
A.3
, for a
C_{M}>0
depending on
M:=\sup\nolimits_{\delta>0}\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})
. As
e(v_{\delta})=0
on
\Omega\setminus\Omega_{\delta}^{\rm good}
and
u_{\delta}=v_{\delta}
on
\Omega\cap\Omega_{\delta}^{\rm good}
, see (
3.21
), by recalling (
3.17
)(i), for
\delta>0
small enough we get a.e. on
V
that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{(V^{\prime}\cap\Omega)\setminus(E^{*}_{\delta}\cup\omega_{u})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e(v_{\delta})=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{(V^{\prime}\cap\Omega_{\delta}^{\rm good}\cap\Omega)\setminus(E^{*}_{\delta}\cup\omega_{u})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e(u_{\delta})=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{(V^{\prime}\cap\Omega)\setminus(E^{*}_{\delta}\cup\omega^{\delta}_{u})}\,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e(u_{\delta}).}

By using (
3.26
) and recalling that by definition
u=v
on
\Omega
, we obtain (
3.7
)(ii) as
V^{\prime}\subset\subset V
was arbitrary. This concludes the proof of (
3.7
)(ii). For later purposes, we also directly discuss the implications of the estimate (
3.27
) in the subsequent remark.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

In the setting of the previous result, we also have

(3.28)

\displaystyle\int_{J_{u}\setminus\partial^{*}E}2\varphi(\nu_{u})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial^{*}E\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega}\varphi(\nu_{E})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+F^{\rm bd\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}(u,E)\leq\liminf_{\delta\to 0}F_{\rm surf}^{\varphi,\gamma_{\delta},q}(E_{\delta})\,,

where
F^{\rm bd\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}
is defined in (
3.8
) and
F_{\rm surf}^{\varphi,\gamma_{\delta},q}
in (
2.1
). Indeed, note that
J_{u}\cap E^{0}=J_{u}\setminus\partial^{*}E
since
u=0
on
E
. Then, by the fact that
u=v
on
\Omega
,
E\subset\Omega
,
V\cap\partial\Omega=\partial_{D}\Omega
, and (
3.24
), we observe

\displaystyle\int\limits_{J_{u}\setminus\partial^{*}E}2\varphi(\nu_{u})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+\hskip-4.26773pt\int\limits_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial^{*}E\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega}\varphi(\nu_{E})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+F^{\rm bd\color[rgb]{0,0,0}r\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}(u,E)=\hskip-4.26773pt\int\limits_{J_{v}\cap E^{0}}\hskip-4.26773pt2\varphi(\nu_{v})\,{\rm d}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}+\hskip-4.26773pt\int\limits_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial^{*}E\cap\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\hskip-4.26773pt\varphi(\nu_{E})\,{\rm d}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\,.

Then, in view of (
3.18
)(ii) and (
3.27
) for a sequence
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}){}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}n\in\mathbb{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\subset\subset V
with
\mathcal{L}^{d}(V\setminus V_{n})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
we get (
3.28
).

Remark 3.10
.

A closer inspection of the previous proof reveals that the compactness result in Proposition
3.1
remains valid, even if we impose
thickened boundary conditions
for the sequence
(u_{\delta})_{\delta>0}
, for instance in the following way.

As before, let
u_{0}\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
d=2,3
. Similarly to the argument in the previous proof, we introduce an open set
V\supset\Omega
such that
V
and
V\setminus\overline{\Omega}
are Lipschitz sets and
V\cap\partial\Omega=\partial_{D}\Omega
. For
\delta>0
define
y_{0}^{\delta}:={\rm id}+\delta u_{0,\delta}
, where
(u_{0,\delta})_{\delta>0}\subset W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{d})
is such that
u_{0,\delta}\to u_{0}
locally uniformly in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
\delta\to 0
. Consider also a sequence of open Lipschitz sets
(V_{\delta})_{\delta>0}
, with
V_{\delta}\subset\subset V\setminus\overline{\Omega}
such that
\chi_{V_{\delta}}\to\chi_{V\setminus\Omega}
locally uniformly. We let again
F_{\delta}(y,E)
be defined by (
3.6
) if
E\in\mathcal{A}_{\rm reg}(\Omega)
,
\overline{E}\cap\partial_{D}\Omega=\emptyset
,
y|_{V\setminus\overline{E}}\in H^{1}(V\setminus\overline{E};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
y|_{E}={\rm id}
, and now
y|_{V_{\delta}}=y^{\delta}_{0}|_{V_{\delta}}
, and
F_{\delta}(y,E)=+\infty
, otherwise. Then the conclusion of Proposition
3.1
still holds.

Proof of Proposition
3.5
.

Consider
(u_{\delta},h_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K:=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sup_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}(u_{\delta},h_{\delta})<+\infty
. First, by this energy bound, (
3.10
), and a standard compactness argument, we find
h\in BV(\omega;[0,M])
such that
h_{\delta}\to h
in
L^{1}(\omega)
, up to a subsequence (not relabeled). For the compactness of
(u_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, we proceed as in the proof of Proposition
3.1
, applied for
V:=\omega\times(-2,M+1)
, i.e.,
U_{D}:=\omega\times(-2,-1\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and
E_{\delta}:=\Omega\setminus\overline{\Omega_{h_{\delta}}}
. The only point to prove is that
\omega_{u}
given in (
3.23
) satisfies
\mathcal{L}^{d}(\omega_{u})=0
. In fact, then (
3.11
) for a limit
u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
follows from (
3.7
). Eventually, since
u_{\delta}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega_{h_{\delta}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{\delta}
and
u_{\delta}=u_{0}
on
\omega\times(-1,0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, by the fact that
\mathcal{G}_{\delta}(u_{\delta},h_{\delta})<+\infty
(see (
3.10
)), (
3.11
)(i) shows
u=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega_{h}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u
and
u=u_{0}
on
\omega\times(-1,0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

Let us now check that
\mathcal{L}^{d}(\omega_{u})=0
. To this end, we apply Corollary
2.2
once again, now in the version for graphs, see Corollary
2.3
. We denote the corresponding set
E^{\prime}_{\eta_{\delta},\gamma_{\delta}}
by
E^{\prime}_{\delta}
for simplicity and we let
h^{\prime}_{\delta}\colon\omega\to\mathbb{R}
be such that
\Omega_{h^{\prime}_{\delta}}=\Omega\setminus\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{E^{\prime}_{\delta}}
. We note that
E^{\prime}_{\delta}\supset E_{\delta}^{*}
and thus
h^{\prime}_{\delta}\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h_{\delta}
. This along with (
2.5
)(i) implies
h^{\prime}_{\delta}\to h
in
L^{1}(\omega)
since
\eta_{\delta},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma_{\delta}\to 0
. In view of (
3.22
), (
2.5
)(ii) applied for
\varphi\equiv 1
, and the fact that
E^{\prime}_{\delta}\supset E_{\delta}^{*}
, we get
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v{}_{\delta}|_{\Omega_{h^{\prime}_{\delta}}}\in H^{1}(\Omega_{h^{\prime}_{\delta}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\sup_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Big(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{\Omega_{h^{\prime}_{\delta}}^{+}}|e(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v{}_{\delta})|^{2}\,{\rm d}x+\int_{\omega}\sqrt{1+|\nabla h^{\prime}_{\delta}|^{2}}\,{\rm d}x^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Big)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}<+\infty\,.

Therefore, by
[
29
, Theorem 2.5]
we find that
u=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega_{h}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
is such that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega_{h^{\prime}_{\delta}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v{}_{\delta}\to u
in measure. (Indeed,
u
coincides with the limiting function identified above.) As
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v{}_{\delta}=0
on
E_{\delta}
and
\mathcal{L}^{d}(E^{\prime}_{\delta}\setminus E_{\delta})\to 0
by (
2.5
)(i), we conclude that
\omega_{u}
defined in (
3.23
) satisfies
\mathcal{L}^{d}(\omega_{u})=0
.
∎

3.4.
Derivation of effective linearized limits by
\Gamma
-convergence

We start with two results on the linearization of nonlinear elastic energies which are by now classical, see e.g.
[
3
,
13
,
32
,
47
,
48
,
75
,
82
,
83
]
. For completeness, however, we include short proofs, in particular due to the fact that our setting, involving varying sets
(E_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, is slightly different compared to the above mentioned works. Recall the quadratic form
\mathcal{Q}
defined in (
3.3
).

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
(u_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\subset GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
,
u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
, and let
(\Theta_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}},\Theta\in\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
be such that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Theta_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e(u_{\delta})\rightharpoonup\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Theta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e(u)
weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\rm sym})
,
\mathcal{L}^{d}(\{|\nabla u_{\delta}|>\kappa_{\delta}\}\cap\Theta)\to 0
, and
u=0
on
\Omega\setminus\Theta
, where
\kappa_{\delta}
is defined in (
3.5
). Then,

\displaystyle\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\int_{\Omega}W({\rm Id}+\delta\nabla u_{\delta})\,{\rm d}x\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{Q}(e(u))\,\mathrm{d}x\,.

Proof.

We define
\vartheta_{\delta}\in L^{\infty}(\Omega)
by
\vartheta_{\delta}(x)=\chi_{[0,\kappa_{\delta}]}(|\nabla u_{\delta}(x)|)
, and note that
\mathcal{L}^{d}(\{|\nabla u_{\delta}|>\kappa_{\delta}\}\cap\Theta)\to 0
implies
\vartheta_{\delta}\to 1
boundedly in measure on
\Theta
, as
\delta\to 0
. By the regularity and the structural hypotheses of
W
we get
W({\rm Id}+F)=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Q}({\rm sym}(F))+\Phi(F)
, where
\Phi\colon\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}\to\mathbb{R}
is a function satisfying
|\Phi(F)|\leq C|F|^{3}
for all
F\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with
|F|\leq 1
. Then, the fact that (
3.5
) implies
\delta\kappa_{\delta}\to 0
and hence
0<\delta\kappa_{\delta}\leq 1
for
\delta>0
sufficiently small, together with the fact that
W\geq 0
, imply that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\int_{\Omega}W({\rm Id}+\delta\nabla u_{\delta})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\int_{\Theta}\vartheta_{\delta}W({\rm Id}+\delta\nabla u_{\delta})\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\int_{\Theta}\vartheta_{\delta}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Q}(e(u_{\delta}))+\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\Phi(\delta\nabla u_{\delta})\Big)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\Big(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1}{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{\Theta}\vartheta_{\delta}Q(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Theta_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e(u_{\delta}))\,{\rm d}x\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-C\int_{\Theta}\vartheta_{\delta}\delta|\nabla u_{\delta}|^{3}\Big)\,.\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

The second term converges to zero since
\vartheta_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}|\nabla u_{\delta}|^{3}
is uniformly controlled from above by
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\kappa_{\delta}^{3}
, where
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\kappa_{\delta}^{3}\to 0
by (
3.5
). As
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Theta_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e(u_{\delta})\rightharpoonup\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Theta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e(u)
weakly in
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}_{\rm sym})
, by the convexity of
\mathcal{Q}
, and the fact that
\vartheta_{\delta}
converges to
1
boundedly in measure on
\Theta
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\int_{\Omega}W(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{Id}+\delta\nabla u_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\,{\rm d}x\geq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1}{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{\Theta}\mathcal{Q}(e(u))\,{\rm d}x=\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Q}(e(u))\,{\rm d}x\,,

where the last step follows from the fact that
u=0
on
\Omega\setminus\Theta
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
(\Theta_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
be a sequence of open subsets of
\Omega
and let
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}){}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\in H^{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Theta_{\delta};\mathbb{R}^{d})
be such that

(3.29)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Theta_{\delta})}\leq\delta^{-1/4}\,.

Then, as
\delta\to 0
, we have for
y_{\delta}:={\rm id}+\delta u_{\delta}
that

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\Big|\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\int_{\Theta_{\delta}}W(\nabla y_{\delta})\,{\rm d}x-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1}{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{\Theta_{\delta}}\mathcal{Q}(e(u_{\delta}))\,{\rm d}x\Big|=0\,.

Proof.

As in the previous proof, we use that
W({\rm Id}+F)=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Q}({\rm sym}(F))+\Phi(F)
with
|\Phi(F)|\leq C|F|^{3}
for
|F|\leq 1
. Then, for
y_{\delta}={\rm id}+\delta u_{\delta}
, we compute

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\int_{\Theta_{\delta}}W(\nabla y_{\delta})\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\int_{\Theta_{\delta}}W({\rm Id}+\delta\nabla u_{\delta})\,{\rm d}x=\int_{\Theta_{\delta}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Q}(e(u_{\delta}))+\frac{1}{\delta^{2}}\Phi(\delta\nabla u_{\delta})\Big)\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Theta_{\delta}}\mathcal{Q}(e(u_{\delta}))\,{\rm d}x+\int_{\Theta_{\delta}}{\rm O}\big(\delta|\nabla u_{\delta}|^{3}\big)\,.

The result now follows by taking limits and using (
3.29
).
∎

We now proceed with the
\Gamma
-convergence results. The proofs essentially rely on the above preparations, the estimates in Subsection
3.3
, and the results in the linearized setting obtained in
[
29
, Section 2]
. We start with Theorem
3.2
.

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

We first address the lower bound and afterwards the upper bound.

Step 1
.(Lower bound)
Suppose that
(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})\overset{\tau}{\to}(u,E)
, i.e., there exist a set of finite perimeter
\omega_{u}\in\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
such that
\chi_{E_{\delta}}\to\chi_{E}\text{ in }L^{1}(\Omega)
,
u_{\delta}\to u
in measure on
\Omega\setminus\omega_{u}
, and
u\equiv 0
on
E\cup\omega_{u}
. Without restriction, we can assume that
\sup_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(u_{\delta},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}E_{\delta})<+\infty
. In view of Proposition
3.1
, this yields
u=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega\setminus E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
,
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}E)<+\infty
, and that (
3.7
) holds. Therefore, we obtain
\mathcal{F}_{0}(u,E)<+\infty
. Now, the lower bound for the surface energy follows directly from Remark
3.9
. For the elastic part, we use (
3.7
)(ii),(iii) and apply Lemma
3.11
for an arbitrary
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\subset\Omega
, for
\Theta_{\delta}=\Omega^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus(E_{\delta}^{*}\cup\omega^{\delta}_{u})
and
\Theta=\Omega^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus(E\cup\omega_{u})
.

Step 2
.(Recovery sequence)
By
[
29
, Theorem 2.2]
, for each
E\in\mathfrak{M}(\Omega)
with
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}E)<+\infty
and each
u=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega\setminus E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
, there exists a sequence of sets
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}E_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}){}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}E_{\delta}\subset\subset\Omega
,
\partial E_{\delta}\in C^{\infty}
,
\chi_{E_{\delta}}\to\chi_{E}
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
and a sequence
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(u_{\delta})_{\delta>0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
with
u_{\delta}|_{\Omega\setminus\overline{E_{\delta}}}\in H^{1}(\Omega\setminus\overline{E_{\delta}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
u_{\delta}|_{E_{\delta}}=0
, and
{\rm tr}(u_{\delta})={\rm tr}(u_{0})
on
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial_{D}\Omega\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
such that
u_{\delta}\to u
in
L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

(3.30)

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\Big(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1}{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\int_{\Omega\setminus\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{E_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\mathcal{Q}(e(u_{\delta}))\,{\rm d}x+\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial E_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\varphi(\nu_{E_{\delta}})\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\Big)=\mathcal{F}_{0}(u,E)\,.

Strictly speaking,
[
29
, Theorem 2.2]
only ensures that
\partial E_{\delta}
is Lipschitz, see
[
29
, (2.2)]
, but in the proof it is shown that
E_{\delta}
can be chosen compactly contained in
\Omega
and
\partial E_{\delta}
of class
C^{\infty}
, see
[
29
, Proposition 5.4]
. By a density argument and the fact that
\Omega
is Lipschitz, without relabeling of functions and sets, it is not restrictive to further assume that each
u_{\delta}
is Lipschitz on
\Omega\setminus\overline{E_{\delta}}
. By a diagonal argument we may further suppose without restriction that

{\|\nabla u_{\delta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega\setminus\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{E_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\leq\delta^{-1/4},\quad\quad\quad\int_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial E_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}|\bm{A}_{\delta}|^{q}\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\gamma^{-1/2}_{\delta}\,,\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

where
\bm{A}_{\delta}
denotes the second fundamental form associated to
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\partial E_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. Here, we use that
\gamma_{\delta}^{-1/2}\to\infty
, see (
3.4
). Then, in view of (
3.6
) and (
3.30
), by applying Lemma
3.12
for
\Theta_{\delta}=\Omega\setminus\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{E_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and by using again that
\gamma_{\delta}\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
, we conclude that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{F}_{\delta}(u_{\delta},E_{\delta})=\lim_{\delta\to 0}{F}_{\delta}(y_{\delta},E_{\delta})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\mathcal{F}_{0}(u,E)
, where
y_{\delta}={\rm id}+\delta u_{\delta}
. This concludes the construction of recovery sequences. Eventually,
[
29
, Theorem 2.2]
also shows that a volume constraint can be incorporated, which yields Remark
3.3
(i).

∎

We now proceed with the proof of Theorem
3.6
. To this end, we recall the notion of
\sigma_{\rm sym}^{2}
-convergence introduced in
[
29
, Section 4]
, in a slightly simplified version. In the following, we use the notation
A\widetilde{\subset}B
if
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(A\setminus B)=0
and
A\widetilde{=}B
if
A\widetilde{\subset}B
and
B\widetilde{\subset}A
.

Definition 3.13

(
\sigma^{2}_{\rm sym}
-convergence)
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open,
U^{\prime}\supset U
be open with
\mathcal{L}^{d}(U^{\prime}\setminus U)>0
. Consider a sequence
(\Gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\overline{U}\cap U^{\prime}
with
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\Gamma_{n})<+\infty
. We suppose that for each
C>0
the sets

(3.31)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}_{C,n}:=\Big\{v\in GSBD^{2}(U^{\prime})\colon\text{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}$v=0$ \color[rgb]{0,0,0}in $U^{\prime}\setminus U$,\, $\|e(v)\|_{L^{2}(U^{\prime})}\leq C$,\, $\color[rgb]{0,0,0}J_{v}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\subset}\Gamma_{n}$}\Big\}}

are equi-precompact in
L^{0}(U^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{d})
, in the sense that every sequence
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with
v_{n}\in\mathcal{X}_{C,n}
admits a convergent subsequence in
L^{0}(U^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then, we say that
(\Gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\sigma_{\rm sym}^{2}
-converges to
\Gamma
satisfying
\Gamma\subset\overline{U}\cap U^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\Gamma)<+\infty
, if there holds:

(i) for any
C>0
and any sequence
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v_{n}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}){}_{n\in\mathbb{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with
v_{n}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{X}_{C,n}
, if a subsequence
(v_{n_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
converges in measure to
v\in GSBD^{2}(U^{\prime})
, then
J_{v}\widetilde{\subset}\Gamma
.

(ii) there exists a function
v\in GSBD^{2}(U^{\prime})
and a sequence
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with
v_{n}\in\mathcal{X}_{C,n}
for some
C>0
such that
v_{n}\to v
in measure on
U^{\prime}
and
J_{v}\widetilde{=}\Gamma
.

Our definition is simplified compared to
[
29
, Section 4]
as we assume a compactness property for the sets in (
3.31
). Indeed, all involved sequences converge in measure on
U^{\prime}
, and therefore we can neglect the set
G_{\infty}
appearing in
[
29
, Definition 4.1]
, which is related to the set where a sequence
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
as in (i) may converge to infinity. In a similar fashion, the space
GSBD^{2}_{\infty}
introduced in
[
29
, Subsection 3.4]
is not needed. Note that imposing boundary conditions in (
3.31
) is fundamental for compactness, by excluding nonzero constant functions. We refer to
[
29
, Section 4]
for a more general discussion on this notion and mention here only the fundamental compactness result, see
[
29
, Theorem 4.2]
.

Theorem 3.14

(Compactness of
\sigma^{2}_{\rm sym}
-convergence)
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be open,
U^{\prime}\supset U
be open with
\mathcal{L}^{d}(U^{\prime}\setminus U)>0
. Then, every sequence
(\Gamma_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\subset\overline{U}\cap U^{\prime}
satisfying the assumptions in Definition
3.13
has a
\sigma_{\rm sym}^{2}
-convergent subsequence (not relabeled) with limit
\Gamma
satisfying the inequality
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\Gamma)\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\Gamma_{n})
.

Moreover, the following lower semicontinuity result can be shown.

Lemma 3.15

(Lower semicontinuity of surfaces)
.

Let
\Omega=\omega\times(-1,M+1)
. Let
(D_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
be a sequence of Lipschitz sets such that
\Gamma_{\delta}:=\partial D_{\delta}\cap\Omega
are
\sigma_{\rm sym}^{2}
-converging to
\Gamma
in the sense of Definition
3.13
with respect to the sets
U=\omega\times(-\frac{1}{2},M)
and
U^{\prime}=\Omega
. Suppose that there exists a function
h\in BV(\omega;[0,M])
such that
\mathcal{L}^{d}((\Omega\setminus D_{\delta})\triangle\Omega_{h})\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}\Omega_{h}\cap\Omega)+2\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\Gamma\cap\Omega_{h}^{1})\leq\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\Gamma_{\delta})\,.

Proof.

For the proof we refer to
[
29
, Subsection 6.1]
, in particular to
[
29
, (6.4), (6.6)]
. Note that there the proof was only performed in the case that
\partial D_{\delta}\cap\Omega
are graphs, but this assumption is not needed since the argument relies on the lower semicontinuity result in
[
29
, Theorem 5.1]
.
∎

We are now in the position to give the proof of Theorem
3.6
.

Proof of Theorem
3.6
.

We first address the lower bound and afterwards the upper bound.

Step 1
.(Lower bound)
Suppose that
u_{\delta}\to u
in
L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
and that
h_{\delta}\to h
in
L^{1}(\omega)
. Without restriction, we can assume that
\sup_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\mathcal{G}_{\delta}(u_{\delta},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}h_{\delta})<+\infty
. By Proposition
3.5
this implies that
h\in BV(\omega;[0,M])
,
u=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega_{h}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
, as well as
u=u_{0}
on
\omega\times(-1,0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. Therefore,
\mathcal{G}_{0}(u,h)<+\infty
. Moreover, (
3.11
) holds. The lower bound for the elastic energy follows by (
3.11
)(ii),(iii) and by Lemma
3.11
applied for
\Theta_{\delta}:=\Omega^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\setminus(E_{\delta}^{*}\cup\omega_{u}^{\delta})
and
\Theta:=\Omega^{\prime}
for arbitrary
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\subset\Omega
. Therefore, it remains to prove that

\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}\Omega_{h}\cap\Omega)+2\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(J_{u}^{\prime}\cap\Omega^{1}_{h})\leq\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\Big(\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\big(\partial\Omega_{h_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega\big)+\gamma_{\delta}\int_{\partial\Omega_{h_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega}|\bm{A}_{\delta}|^{q}\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\Big)\,,

where
\bm{A}_{\delta}
denotes the second fundamental form corresponding to
\partial\Omega_{h_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and
J_{u}^{\prime}
is defined in (
3.13
). To this end, we define

\Gamma_{\delta}:=\partial E_{\delta}^{*}\cap\Omega\,,

where
(E_{\delta}^{*})_{\delta>0}
are given in (
3.11
), and note that
\sup_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\Gamma_{\delta})<+\infty
by (
3.11
)(iv) (up to a subsequence, not relabeled). We let
U=\omega\times(-\frac{1}{2},M)
and
U^{\prime}=\Omega=\omega\times(-1,M+1)
. By
[
29
, Theorem 2.5]
and Corollary
2.3
for
\varphi\equiv 1
, we now observe that the sets given in (
3.31
) are equi-precompact in
L^{0}(U^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In fact, given
v_{\delta}\in\mathcal{X}_{C,\delta}
, we define
w_{\delta}:=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Omega\setminus\overline{E_{\delta}^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v_{\delta}
, where
\partial E_{\delta}^{\prime}\cap\Omega
is the graph of a function, see Corollary
2.3
. By (
2.5
)(ii) and the fact that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\sup_{\delta>0}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|e(w_{\delta})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}<+\infty\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, we can apply
[
29
, Theorem 2.5]
to find that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{\delta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}){}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
converges in measure on
\Omega
to some
w\in L^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By (
2.5
)(i) we conclude
v_{\delta}\to w
in measure, as well. Therefore, as
(\mathcal{X}_{C,\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
are equi-precompact, we can apply Theorem
3.14
to deduce that
(\Gamma_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}

\sigma_{\rm sym}^{2}
-converges (up to a subsequence) to some
\Gamma\subset\overline{U}\cap U^{\prime}
. By combining (
3.11
)(iv) and Lemma
3.15
for
D_{\delta}=E_{\delta}^{*}
(note that indeed
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{L}^{d}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}((\Omega\setminus E^{*}_{\delta})\triangle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Omega_{h})\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
by Proposition
3.5
) we get

(3.32)

\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\partial^{*}\Omega_{h}\cap\Omega)+2\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(\Gamma\cap\Omega_{h}^{1})\leq\liminf_{\delta\to 0}\Big(\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\big(\partial\Omega_{h_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega\big)+\gamma_{\delta}\int_{\partial\Omega_{h_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega}|\bm{A}_{\delta}|^{q}\,{\rm d}\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\Big)\,.

Thus, to conclude the proof, it remains to check that
J_{u}^{\prime}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cap\Omega_{h}^{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\subset\Gamma\cap\Omega_{h}^{1}
up to an
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
-negligible set. To this end, we follow
[
29
, Subsection 6.1]
: consider the sequence of mappings
v_{\delta}:=\psi\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega\setminus E^{*}_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u_{\delta}
, where
\psi\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
\psi=1
in a neighborhood of
\Omega^{+}=\Omega\cap\{x_{d}>0\}
and
\psi=0
on
\omega\times(-1,-\frac{1}{2})
. Moreover, for
t>0
, we let
v^{\prime}_{\delta}(x):=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega\setminus E^{*}_{\delta}}(x)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}v_{\delta}(x^{\prime},x_{d}-t)
, extended by zero in
\omega{\times}(-1,-1+t)
. Defining
v^{\prime}(x)=\psi\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega_{h}}(x)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u(x^{\prime},x_{d}-t)
, we observe that
v^{\prime}_{\delta}
converge to
v^{\prime}
in measure on
U^{\prime}
since
u_{\delta}\to u
in measure on
U^{\prime}
, see (
3.11
)(i). We also observe that
v^{\prime}_{\delta}=0
on
U^{\prime}\setminus U=\omega\times((-1,-\frac{1}{2}]\cup[M,M+1))
. Thus, applying Definition
3.13
(i) on the sequence
(v^{\prime}_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
, which clearly satisfies
J_{v^{\prime}_{\delta}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\subset}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{\delta}
, we obtain
J_{v^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{\subset}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma
. This shows

(J_{u}+te_{d})\cap\Omega_{h}^{1}=J_{v^{\prime}}\cap\Omega_{h}^{1}\subset\Gamma\cap\Omega_{h}^{1}\,.

Since
t\geq 0
was arbitrary, recalling the definition of
J_{u}^{\prime}=\{(x^{\prime},x_{d}+t)\colon\,x\in J_{u},\,t\geq 0\}
, see (
3.13
), we indeed find
J_{u}^{\prime}\cap\Omega^{1}_{h}\subset\Gamma\cap\Omega_{h}^{1}
. In view of (
3.32
), this concludes the proof of the lower bound.

Step 2
.(Recovery sequence)
By applying
[
29
, Theorem 2.4]
, for each
h\in BV(\omega;[0,M])
and for each
u=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\chi_{\Omega_{h}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}u\in GSBD^{2}(\Omega)
with
u=u_{0}
on
\omega\times(-1,0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
there exists a sequence
(h_{\delta})_{\delta\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\subset C^{\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\omega;[0,M])
and mappings
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(u_{\delta}){}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\delta>0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}
with
u_{\delta}|_{\Omega_{h_{\delta}}}\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}H^{1}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\Omega_{h_{\delta}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
u_{\delta}=0
on
\Omega\setminus\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\overline{\Omega_{h_{\delta}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, and
u_{\delta}=u_{0}
on
\omega\times(-1,0\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
such that
h_{\delta}\to h
in
